Computation of Weighted Bergman Inner Products on Bounded Symmetric Domains and Restriction to Subgroups
Ryosuke NAKAHAMA

Abstract
Let ( G , G 1 ) (G,G_{1}) be a symmetric pair of holomorphic type, and we consider a pair of Hermitian symmetric spaces D 1 = G 1 / K 1 ⊂ D = G / K D_{1}=G_{1}/K_{1}\subset D=G/K , realized as bounded symmetric domains in complex vector spaces 𝔭 1 + ⊂ 𝔭 + \mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+} respectively. Then the universal covering group G ~ \widetilde{G} of G G acts unitarily on the weighted Bergman space ℋ λ ​ ( D ) ⊂ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) \mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)\subset\mathcal{O}(D) on D D . Its restriction to the subgroup G ~ 1 \widetilde{G}_{1} decomposes discretely and multiplicity-freely, and its branching law is given explicitly by Hua–Kostant–Schmid–Kobayashi’s formula in terms of the K 1 K_{1} -decomposition of the space 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝔭 2 + ) \mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}) of polynomials on the orthogonal complement 𝔭 2 + \mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2} of 𝔭 1 + \mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1} in 𝔭 + \mathfrak{p}^{+} . The object of this article is to compute explicitly the inner product ⟨ f ⁡ ( x 2 ) , e ( x | z ¯ ) 𝔭 + ⟩ λ \big\langle f(x_{2}),{\rm e}^{(x|\overline{z})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}}\big\rangle_{\lambda} for f ⁡ ( x 2 ) ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( 𝔭 2 + ) f(x_{2})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}) , x = ( x 1 , x 2 ) x=(x_{1},x_{2}) , z ∈ 𝔭 + = 𝔭 1 + ⊕ 𝔭 2 + z\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2} . For example, when 𝔭 + \mathfrak{p}^{+} , 𝔭 2 + \mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2} are of tube type and f ⁡ ( x 2 ) = det ⁡ ( x 2 ) k f(x_{2})=\operatorname{det}(x_{2})^{k} , we compute this inner product explicitly by introducing a multivariate generalization of Gauss’ hypergeometric polynomials F 1 2 {}_{2}F_{1} . Also, as an application, we construct explicitly G ~ 1 \widetilde{G}_{1} -intertwining operators (symmetry breaking operators) ℋ λ ​ ( D ) | G ~ 1 → ℋ μ ​ ( D 1 ) \mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mu}(D_{1}) from holomorphic discrete series representations of G ~ \widetilde{G} to those of G ~ 1 \widetilde{G}_{1} , which are unique up to constant multiple for sufficiently large λ \lambda .



keywords
weighted Bergman spaces; holomorphic discrete series representations; branching laws; intertwining operators; symmetry breaking operators; highest weight modules

22E45; 43A85; 17C30; 33C67

1
Introduction

The purpose of this article is to compute explicitly the weighted Bergman inner product of polynomials on some subspace
of a bounded symmetric domain, and study the decomposition of the restriction of a holomorphic discrete series representation
to some subgroup in detail.

Let
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
be a finite-dimensional complex vector space,
D\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
be a Hermitian symmetric space realized as a bounded symmetric domain
centered at the origin,
G
be the indentity component of the biholomorphism group of
D
,
and
K\subset G
be the isotropy subgroup at the origin,
so that
D\simeq G/K
holds. Let
\widetilde{G}
and
\widetilde{K}
denote the universal covering groups of
G
and
K
respectively.
We consider an irreducible unitary representation
(\tau,V)
of
\widetilde{K}
, and consider the homogeneous vector bundle

\widetilde{G}\times_{\widetilde{K}}V\to\widetilde{G}/\widetilde{K}\simeq D
.
Then this bundle is trivializable, and the space of holomorphic sections is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(D,V)
,
the space of
V
-valued holomorphic functions on
D
, on which
\widetilde{G}
acts by

(\hat{\tau}(g)f)(x):=\tau\big(\kappa\big(g^{-1},x\big)\big)^{-1}f\big(g^{-1}x\big),\qquad g\in\widetilde{G},\quad x\in D,\quad f\in\mathcal{O}(D,V),

by using a function
\kappa\colon\widetilde{G}\times D\to\widetilde{K}^{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying the cocycle condition.
Next we consider a function
B\colon D\times\overline{D}\to\widetilde{K}^{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying

B(gx,\overline{gy})=\kappa(g,x)B(x,\overline{y})\kappa(g,y)^{*},\qquad g\in\widetilde{G},\quad x,y\in D.

Then the function
\tau(B(x,\overline{y}))\in\mathcal{O}\big(D\times\overline{D},\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)\big)\simeq\mathcal{O}\big(D\times\overline{D},V\otimes\overline{V}\big)

is invariant under the diagonal action of
\widetilde{G}
, and if this is positive definite, then there exists a Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(D,V)\subset\mathcal{O}(D,V)
with the reproducing kernel
\tau(B(x,\overline{y}))
, on which
\widetilde{G}
acts unitarily.
We call the representation
(\hat{\tau},\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(D,V))
a
unitary highest weight representation
of
\widetilde{G}
.
Such representations are classified by
[
7
,
15
]
. Moreover, if the inner product is given by the converging integral

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{\hat{\tau}}:=C_{\tau}\int_{D}\big(\tau\big(B(x,\overline{x})^{-1}\big)f(x),g(x)\big)_{\tau}\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(B(x,\overline{x}))^{-1}{\rm d}x

(1.1)

(a
weighted Bergman inner product
), then
(\hat{\tau},\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(D,V))
is called a
holomorphic discrete series representation
.
In this paper, when
(\tau,V)
is written as
(\tau,V)=\big(\chi^{-\lambda}\otimes\tau_{0},V\big)
by using a fixed character
\chi

and a fixed representation
(\tau_{0},V)
of
\widetilde{K}
, we also write
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(D,V)=:\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D,V)
,
\mathcal{O}(D,V)=:\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(D,V)
.
In addition, if
V=\mathbb{C}
then we write
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D,\mathbb{C})=:\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)
and call it of
scalar type
.
If
\lambda
is sufficiently large, then
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D,V)
is holomorphic discrete, and the
\widetilde{K}
-finite part

\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D,V)_{\widetilde{K}}
coincides with the space of all polynomials
\mathcal{O}(D,V)_{\widetilde{K}}=\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+},V)
.
On the other hand, for smaller
\lambda
,
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(D,V)_{\widetilde{K}}=\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+},V)
may be reducible as a
\big(\mathfrak{g},\widetilde{K}\big)
-module,
and
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D,V)_{\widetilde{K}}
may be smaller than
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+},V)
even if it exists.
By computing the inner product (
1.1
) for large
\lambda
and observing the poles of its meromorphic continuation,
we can get some information on submodules of
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(D,V)_{\widetilde{K}}
(see, e.g.,
[
10
,
32
]
).

For example, we consider the bounded symmetric domain
D
of type
I\!I\!I_{r}
,

D:=\{x\in\operatorname{Sym}(r,\mathbb{C})\mid I-xx^{*}\text{ is positive definite}\}.

Then the universal covering group
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(r,\mathbb{R})
of

\operatorname{Sp}(r,\mathbb{R}):=\left\{g\in{\rm GL}(2r,\mathbb{C})\ \middle|\ {}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptg\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
-I&0\end{pmatrix}g=\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
-I&0\end{pmatrix}\!,\;g\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{pmatrix}\overline{g}\right\}

acts on
\mathcal{O}(D)=\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(D)
by

\left(\tau_{\lambda}\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\right)f\right)(x)=\operatorname{det}(cx+d)^{-\lambda}f\big((ax+b)(cx+d)^{-1}\big).

We note that
\operatorname{det}(cx+d)^{-\lambda}
is not well-defined on
\operatorname{Sp}(r,\mathbb{R})\times D
if
\lambda\notin\mathbb{Z}
,
but is well-defined on the universal covering space
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(r,\mathbb{R})\times D
.
If
\lambda>r
, then this preserves the weighted Bergman inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\lambda}:=C_{\lambda}\int_{D}f(x)\overline{g(x)}\operatorname{det}(I-xx^{*})^{\lambda-(r+1)}{\rm d}x,

and the corresponding Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)\subset\mathcal{O}(D)
, with the reproducing kernel
\operatorname{det}(I-xy^{*})^{-\lambda}
,
gives a holomorphic discrete series representation of scalar type of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Sp}}(r,\mathbb{R})
.

Next we consider a connected symmetric subgroup
G_{1}\subset G
.
Without loss of generality we may assume
K_{1}:=G_{1}\cap K
is a maximal compact subgroup of
G_{1}
.
Then
D_{1}:=G_{1}.0\simeq G_{1}/K_{1}
is either a complex submanifold or a totally real submanifold of
D\simeq G/K
.

(G,G_{1})
is called a symmetric pair of
holomorphic type
for the former case,
and of
anti-holomorphic type
for the latter case (see
[
19
, Section 3.4]
).
From now on we assume
(G,G_{1})
is a symmetric pair of holomorphic type.
We take a complex subspace
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
such that
D_{1}=D\cap\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}
holds,
and let
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
be the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}
with respect to a suitable inner product of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
Then for any holomorphic discrete series representation
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(D,V)
of
\widetilde{G}
,
the restriction to the subgroup
\widetilde{G}_{1}
decomposes into a Hilbert direct sum of irreducible representations of
\widetilde{G}_{1}
,
and each subrepresentation is generated by a
\widetilde{K}_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})\otimes\big(V|_{\widetilde{K}_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}}\big)
,
where
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
denotes the space of polynomials on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
.
That is, if
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})\otimes\big(V|_{\widetilde{K}_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}}\big)
is decomposed under
\widetilde{K}_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}
as

\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})\otimes\big(V|_{\widetilde{K}_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}}\big)\simeq\bigoplus_{j}m(\tau,\rho_{j})(\rho_{j},W_{j}),\qquad m(\tau,\rho_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

then
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(D,V)
is decomposed under
\widetilde{G}_{1}
abstractly as

\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(D,V)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}\simeq\sideset{}{{}^{\oplus}}{\sum}_{j}m(\tau,\rho_{j})\mathcal{H}_{\rho_{j}}(D_{1},W_{j})

(see Kobayashi
[
19
, Lemma 8.8]
,
[
18
, Section 8]
. For earlier results, see also
[
16
,
30
]
).
Especially, if
(\tau,V)=\big(\chi^{-\lambda},\mathbb{C}\big)
is 1-dimensional,
then since
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
decomposes multiplicity-freely (i.e.,
m(\tau,\rho_{j})\leq 1
holds),

\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}
also decomposes multiplicity-freely.
For example, if
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
is simple, we write the decomposition of
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
under
K_{1}
as

\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r_{2}}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}),

where
\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r_{2}}:=\{\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{r_{2}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r_{2}}\mid k_{1}\geq\cdots\geq k_{r_{2}}\geq 0\}
.
We fix characters
\chi
and
\chi_{1}
of
\widetilde{K}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\widetilde{K}^{\mathbb{C}}_{1}
respectively,
and let
\chi|_{\widetilde{K}^{\mathbb{C}}_{1}}=\chi_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}
, where
\varepsilon_{1}\in\{1,2\}
.
Then the holomorphic discrete series representation
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)
of
\widetilde{G}

is decomposed multiplicity-freely under
\widetilde{G}_{1}
as

\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}\simeq\sideset{}{{}^{\oplus}}{\sum}_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r_{2}}}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon_{1}\lambda}(D_{1},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}))

(1.2)

(see Kobayashi
[
19
, Theorem 8.3]
).
We note that if
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)
is not holomorphic discrete and
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)_{\widetilde{K}}\subsetneq\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
holds,
then (
1.2
) does not hold in general.

In the following we consider holomorphic discrete series representations of scalar type
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)
of
\widetilde{G}
.
In order to understand the above decomposition (
1.2
) concretely, we want to compute explicitly the inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\hat{\tau}}=\langle f,g\rangle_{\lambda}
of
f\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
and
g\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
.
To do this, since
{\rm e}^{\left(x\middle|\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}}g(z)\bigr|_{z=0}=g(x)
holds
for any polynomial
g\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
, it is enough to compute when
g(x)={\rm e}^{(x|\overline{z})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}}
.
Therefore, in this article we aim to compute the inner product

\big\langle f(x_{2}),{\rm e}^{(x|\overline{z})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}}\big\rangle_{\lambda,x},\qquad f\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}),\quad x=(x_{1},x_{2}),\quad z\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}.

(1.3)

This inner product is given by an explicit integral for sufficiently large
\lambda
,
and once we compute this explicitly, then we can consider the meromorphic continuation for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.
By observing the poles and their orders of the inner product (
1.3
),
we can determine which
\big(\mathfrak{g},\widetilde{K}\big)
-submodule of
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(D)_{\widetilde{K}}
contains the
\big(\mathfrak{g}_{1},\widetilde{K}_{1}\big)
-submodule
generated by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
.

Next we consider
\widetilde{G}_{1}
-intertwining operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\downarrow}_{\lambda,W}\colon\quad\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon_{1}\lambda}(D_{1},W)\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(D)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon_{1}\lambda}(D_{1},W),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\uparrow}_{\lambda,W}\colon\quad\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon_{1}\lambda}(D_{1},W)\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon_{1}\lambda}(D_{1},W)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(D)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}.

\mathcal{F}^{\downarrow}_{\lambda,W}
is called a
symmetry breaking operator
,
and
\mathcal{F}^{\uparrow}_{\lambda,W}
is called a
holographic operator
, according to the terminology introduced in
[
24
,
25
]
and
[
26
]
respectively.
By the multiplicity-freeness
[
19
, Theorem A]
, such intertwining operators
\mathcal{F}^{\downarrow}_{\lambda,W}
,

\mathcal{F}^{\uparrow}_{\lambda,W}
between unitary highest weight representations of scalar type are unique up to scalar multiple.
Hence we want to construct these operators explicitly.
Such problem is proposed by T. Kobayashi from the viewpoint of the representation theory (see
[
21
]
),
and studied from various viewpoints. For example, symmetry breaking operators given by differential operators are studied
in the context of automorphic forms, conformal geometry and representation theory by, e.g.,
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
13
,
17
,
20
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
.
Especially, in
[
20
,
24
]
it is proved that the symbols of differential symmetry breaking operators are characterized as
polynomial solutions of certain systems of partial differential equations in general settings (F-method),
and symmetry breaking operators between the spaces of holomorphic sections for homogeneous vector bundles on
D
and
D_{1}
are always given by differential operators.
Also, holographic operators for holomorphic discrete series representations are studied by, e.g.,
[
26
,
33
]
,
and integral intertwining operators for principal series or complementary series representations are studied by, e.g.,
[
27
,
28
,
31
]
.
In this article we construct differential symmetry breaking operators
\mathcal{F}^{\downarrow}_{\lambda,W}

for holomorphic discrete series representations by using the author’s previous result
[
33
, Theorem 3.10]
, which claims that
the differential operator

\mathcal{F}^{\downarrow}_{\lambda,W}\colon\quad\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(D)|_{\widetilde{G}_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon_{1}\lambda}(D_{1},W),\qquad f(x)=f(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto F^{\downarrow}_{\lambda,W}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\bigg)f(x)\bigg|_{x_{2}=0}

defined by using the polynomial
F^{\downarrow}_{\lambda,W}(z)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\otimes W
,

F^{\downarrow}_{\lambda,W}(z):=\big\langle{\rm e}^{(x|z)_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}},\mathrm{K}(x_{2})\big\rangle_{\lambda,x},\qquad\mathrm{K}(x_{2})\in\big(\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})\otimes\overline{W}\big)^{K_{1}}

(1.4)

becomes a symmetry breaking operator. Moreover, by considering the meromorphic continuation with respect to
\lambda
,
this gives a symmetry breaking operator also for non-discrete highest weight representations.
That is, the construction of differential symmetry breaking operators is reduced to the
computation of the inner products (
1.3
), and conversely,
we can use differential equations (F-method) to compute the inner products (
1.3
) up to constant multiple.
However, for non-unitary case we do not know a priori whether the symmetry breaking operators are unique or not,
and for uniqueness we need further study as in
[
24
,
25
]
.
In fact, for tensor product case the space of symmetry breaking operators sometimes becomes 2-dimensional, even for scalar case (see
[
25
, Section 9]
).

When
(G,G_{1})
is of the form
(G_{1}\times G_{1},\Delta G_{1})
, the computation of the inner product (
1.3
)
and the construction of symmetry breaking operators are studied in
[
33
, Section 5.2]
,
[
36
]
(see also, e.g.,
[
3
,
4
,
25
,
26
,
34
,
35
]
).
In the following we consider the case
G
is simple, and for a while we assume
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
are of tube type
(i.e., the corresponding Lie groups
G
and
G_{2}:=(G^{\sigma\vartheta})_{0}
are of tube type, where
\sigma
,
\vartheta
are suitable involutions on
G

satisfying
G_{1}=(G^{\sigma})_{0}
,
K=G^{\vartheta}
),
so that
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\mathfrak{n}^{+\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=\mathfrak{n}^{+\mathbb{C}}_{2}
have complex Jordan algebra structures.
Let
\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{2}}(x_{2})
be the determinant polynomial on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=\mathfrak{n}^{+\mathbb{C}}_{2}
,
so that
\mathbb{C}\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{2}}(x_{2})^{k}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
gives a 1-dimensional
K_{1}
-submodule.
In this article, when
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
is a direct sum of
two simple Jordan subalgebras, that is, when
(G,G_{1})
is one of

\displaystyle({\rm SO}_{0}(2,d+2),{\rm SO}_{0}(2,d)\times{\rm SO}(2)),\qquad(\operatorname{Sp}(r,\mathbb{R}),{\rm U}(r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime})),

\displaystyle({\rm U}(r,r),{\rm U}(r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime})\times{\rm U}(r^{\prime\prime},r^{\prime})),\qquad({\rm SO}^{*}(4r),{\rm U}(2r^{\prime},2r^{\prime\prime})),\qquad(E_{7(-25)},{\rm U}(1)\times E_{6(-14)}),

for
f(x_{2})=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})^{k}f_{2}(x_{22})\in\mathcal{P}_{(k,\dots,k)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11})\boxtimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{l}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22})
,
we compute the inner product

\big\langle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})^{k}f_{2}(x_{22}),{\rm e}^{(x|\overline{z})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}}\big\rangle_{\lambda,x},

and compute the poles and their orders with respect to the parameter
\lambda
(Theorem
5.1
and Corollary
5.7
).
Especially if
f(x_{2})=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})^{k}\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{22}}(x_{22})^{l}
,
then the result is given by using a multivariate generalization of Gauss’ hypergeometric polynomials
{}_{2}F_{1}
,
which coincides with a special case of Heckman–Opdam’s hypergeometric polynomials of type
BC

(Corollary
5.4
and Theorem
5.9
).
Similarly, when
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
is a simple Jordan algebra, that is, when
(G,G_{1})
is one of

\displaystyle({\rm SO}_{0}(2,n),{\rm SO}_{0}(2,n^{\prime})\times{\rm SO}(n^{\prime\prime})),\qquad

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Sp}(2r^{\prime},\mathbb{R}),\operatorname{Sp}(r^{\prime},\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{Sp}(r^{\prime},\mathbb{R})),

\displaystyle({\rm SO}^{*}(4s^{\prime}),{\rm SO}^{*}(2s^{\prime})\times{\rm SO}^{*}(2s^{\prime})),\qquad

\displaystyle({\rm SU}(r,r),{\rm SO}^{*}(2r)),

\displaystyle({\rm SU}(2s,2s),\operatorname{Sp}(2s,\mathbb{R})),\qquad

\displaystyle(E_{7(-25)},{\rm SU}(2,6)),

n^{\prime\prime}\neq 2
, for
f(x_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{(k+l,k,\dots,k)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
(for the 1st, 3rd, 4th and 6th cases) or
for
f(x_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{(k+1,\dots,k+1,k,\dots,k)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
(for the 2nd and 5th cases), we compute the inner product

\big\langle f(x_{2}),{\rm e}^{(x|\overline{z})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}}\big\rangle_{\lambda,x},

and compute the poles and their orders (Theorem
6.3
and Corollary
6.6
).
Especially if
l=0
and
f(x_{2})=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{2}}(x_{2})^{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{(k,\dots,k)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
,
then the result is again given by multivariate
{}_{2}F_{1}
, and if
\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{R}}G=2
with general
l
, then the result is given by ordinary
{}_{2}F_{1}

(Corollary
6.5
and Theorem
6.8
).
The above two lists exhaust all symmetric pairs of holomorphic type such that
G
is simple and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
are of tube type
(for the classification without tube type assumption see
[
19
, Table 3.4.1]
).
Moreover, by the
\widetilde{K}_{1}
-equivariance, the results on poles are generalized for
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
of non-tube type,
and for
f(x_{2})\in\mathcal{P}_{(k,\dots,k,0,\dots,0)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11})\boxtimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{l}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22})
,
\mathcal{P}_{(k+l,k,\dots,k,0,\dots,0)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})

or
\mathcal{P}_{(k+1,\dots,k+1,k,\dots,k,0,\dots,0)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
(Corollaries
5.7
,
6.6
).
These results are applied to construct symmetry breaking operators explicitly.
When
(G,G_{1})=({\rm SO}_{0}(2,n),{\rm SO}_{0}(2,n-1))
, the resulting operators coincide with the holomorphic version of Juhl’s operators
(see
[
17
]
,
[
25
, Section 6]
),
and when
(G,G_{1})=(\operatorname{Sp}(r,\mathbb{R}),\operatorname{Sp}(r^{\prime},\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{Sp}(r^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{R}))

(r=r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime})
, the resulting operators for scalar type representations
coincide with the ones in
[
25
, Section 7]
(for
r^{\prime}=1
case) and
[
13
]
(for
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime\prime}
case) (see also Remark
8.8
).

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
,
we review Jordan triple systems, Jordan algebras and holomorphic discrete series representations.
In Section
3
, we fix the explicit realization of Jordan triple systems.
In Section
4
, we rewrite the inner product (
1.3
) in a differential expression
by using the inverse Laplace transform on Jordan algebras (Theorem
4.4
). This formula is regarded as an analogue of
the Rodrigues formulas for Jacobi polynomials (for such formulas for symmetry breaking operators in tensor product case, see also
[
4
]
).
In Section
5
, we compute (
1.3
) when
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
is a direct sum of two
Jordan triple systems, and in Section
6
, we compute (
1.3
) when
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
is a simple Jordan triple system.
In Section
7
, we apply the results on poles to determining which
(\mathfrak{g},\widetilde{K})
-submodule of

\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(D)_{\widetilde{K}}
contains the
(\mathfrak{g}_{1},\widetilde{K}_{1})
-submodule generated by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2})
for singular
\lambda
.
In Section
8
, we construct the symmetry breaking operators explicitly by using (
1.4
),
which are unique up to scalar multiple, and for convenience we also write the author’s previous results
[
33
]
on holographic operators.

2
Preliminaries 1: General theory

In this section we review Jordan triple systems, Jordan algebras and holomorphic discrete series representations.
For detail see, e.g.,
[
9
, Parts III and V]
,
[
11
,
29
,
38
]
.

2.1
Hermitian positive Jordan triple systems

Let
(\mathfrak{p}^{+},\mathfrak{p}^{-},\{\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\},\overline{\cdot})
be a
Hermitian positive Jordan triple system
, that is,

\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
be finite-dimensional vector spaces over
\mathbb{C}
, with a non-degenerate pairing

(\cdot|\cdot)_{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}\to\mathbb{C}
,

\{\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\}\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\to\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-trilinear map satisfying

\displaystyle\{x,y,z\}=\{z,y,x\},

\displaystyle\{u,v,\{x,y,z\}\}=\{\{u,v,x\},y,z\}-\{x,\{v,u,y\},z\}+\{x,y,\{u,v,z\}\},

\displaystyle(\{u,v,x\}|y)_{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}=(x|\{v,u,y\})_{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}

for any
u,x,z\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
,
v,y\in\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}
, and
\overline{\cdot}\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\to\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear involutive isomorphism such that

(x|\overline{x})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\geq 0
holds for any
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
. We define
D\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
,

Q\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\mp},\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
by

D(x,y)z=Q(x,z)y=\{x,y,z\},

and we write
Q(x):=\frac{1}{2}Q(x,x)
. Also let
B\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
be the
Bergman operator
given by

B(x,y):=I_{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}-D(x,y)+Q(x)Q(y),

let
h=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}\to\mathbb{C}
be the
generic norm
, which is a polynomial on
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}
,
and write
B(x)\!:=B(x,\overline{x})
,
h(x)\!:=h(x,\overline{x})
. Then the
bounded symmetric domain

D=D_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\!\subset\!\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is given by

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=(\text{connected component of }\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}\mid B(x)\text{ is positive definite}\}\text{ which contains }0)

\displaystyle=(\text{connected component of }\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}\mid h(x)>0\}\text{ which contains }0),

(2.1)

and its
Bergman–Shilov boundary

\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is given by

\Sigma=\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}\mid B(x)=0\}.

For
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}
, we say
(x,y)
is
quasi-invertible

if
B(x,y)\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
is invertible, and then the
quasi-inverse

x^{y}\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
is given by

x^{y}:=B(x,y)^{-1}(x-Q(x)y)\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}.

An element
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is called a
tripotent
if it satisfies
\{e,\overline{e},e\}=2e
.
Then
D(e,\overline{e})\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
has eigenvalues 0, 1, 2. For a tripotent
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
we write

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{j}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(\overline{e})_{j}:=\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}\mid D(e,\overline{e})x=jx\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{-}(\overline{e})_{j}=\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{j}:=\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}\mid D(\overline{e},e)x=jx\},\qquad j\in\{0,1,2\},

(2.2)

so that
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}=\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}(e)_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}(e)_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}(e)_{0}
holds (
Peirce decomposition
). Then we have

\displaystyle\{\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{j},\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{k},\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{l}\}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{j-k+l},\qquad j,k,l\in\{0,1,2\},

\displaystyle\{\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2},\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{0},\mathfrak{p}^{+}\}=\{\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0},\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{2},\mathfrak{p}^{+}\}=0,

where for the first formula we regard the right hand side as zero if
j-k+l\notin\{0,1,2\}
.
Especially
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{j}

(j=0,1,2)
are Jordan triple subsystems of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
A non-zero tripotent
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is called
primitive
when
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}=\mathbb{C}e
, and is called
maximal
when
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0}=\{0\}
.
Then the set of all maximal tripotents in
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
coincides with the Bergman–Shilov boundary
\Sigma
.
We call
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is of
tube type
if
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}
holds for some (or equivalently any) maximal tripotent
e
.
Especially
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
is always a Jordan triple system of tube type.
Throughout the paper we assume that the pairing
(\cdot|\cdot)_{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}\to\mathbb{C}
is normalized such that

(e|\overline{e})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}=(\overline{e}|e)_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}=1
holds for any primitive tripotent
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.

A maximal set of primitive tripotents
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
satisfying
D(e_{i},\overline{e}_{j})=0

(i\neq j)
is called
a
Jordan frame
of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
. Then
r
is called the
rank
of the Jordan triple system
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
, and
e=\sum_{j=1}^{r}e_{j}
becomes
a maximal tripotent. When
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is simple, we define integers
d
,
b
,
p
,
n
by

\displaystyle d:=\dim(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e_{i})_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e_{j})_{1}),\quad i\neq j,\qquad b:=\dim(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e_{j})_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{1}),

\displaystyle p:=2+d(r-1)+b,\qquad n:=\dim\mathfrak{p}^{+}=r+\frac{d}{2}r(r-1)+br.

(2.3)

We note that if
r=1
, then
d
is not determined uniquely, and any number is allowed.
Then
h(x,y)
and
B(x,y)
,
(x|y)_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}
and
D(x,y)
are related as

h(x,y)^{p}=\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}B(x,y),\qquad p(x|y)_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}D(x,y),\qquad x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+},\quad y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}.

2.2
Jordan algebras

In this section we fix a tripotent
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
, and consider
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}(e)_{2}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
as in (
2.2
). Then

Q(\overline{e})\colon\ \mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{2},\qquad Q(e)\colon\ \mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{2}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}

are bijective and mutually inverse, and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
has a Jordan algebra structure with the product

x\cdot y:=\frac{1}{2}\{x,\overline{e},y\},

that is,

x\cdot y=y\cdot x,\qquad x^{\mathit{2}}\cdot(x\cdot y)=x\cdot\big(x^{\mathit{2}}\cdot y\big)

holds for any
x,y\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
, where
x^{\mathit{2}}:=x\cdot x
. The unit element is
e
, and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
has the Euclidean real form

\mathfrak{n}^{+}:=\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\mid Q(e)\overline{x}=x\}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}.

Let
(\cdot|\cdot)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}
be the symmetric bilinear form on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}=\mathfrak{n}^{+\mathbb{C}}
given by

(x|y)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}:=(x|Q(\overline{e})y)_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}=(y|Q(\overline{e})x)_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}},\qquad x,y\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}.

Then this is positive definite on
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
. Also let
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x):=(x|e)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}
,
let
\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)
be the
determinant polynomial
on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}=\mathfrak{n}^{+\mathbb{C}}
,
and let
\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{-}}(y)=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(Q(e)y)
for
y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{2}
.

Next, let
L\colon\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})
be the multiplication operator, that is,

L(x)y:=x\cdot y=\frac{1}{2}D(x,\overline{e})y,

and let
P\colon\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})
,
P,D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}},B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\colon\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\times\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})

and
h_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\colon\allowbreak\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\times\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
be the maps given by

\displaystyle P(x):=2L(x)^{2}-L\big(x^{\mathit{2}}\big)=Q(x)Q(\overline{e}),

\displaystyle P(x,y):=2(L(x)L(y)+L(y)L(x)-L(x\cdot y))=Q(x,y)Q(\overline{e}),

\displaystyle D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x,y):=2(L(x\cdot y)+L(x)L(y)-L(y)L(x))=D(x,Q(\overline{e})y),

\displaystyle B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x,y):=I_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}}-D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x,y)+P(x)P(y)=B(x,Q(\overline{e})y),

\displaystyle h_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x,y):=h(x,Q(\overline{e})y).

Then for any
x,y,z,w\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
we have

\displaystyle P(P(x)y)=P(x)P(y)P(x),\qquad P(B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x,y)z)=B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x,y)P(z)B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(y,x),

\displaystyle(P(x,z)y|w)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}=(P(y,w)x|z)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}},

and if
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
is simple, then the determinant polynomial
\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)
and
P(x)
are related as

\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)^{\frac{2n^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}}}=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)^{2+d(r^{\prime}-1)}=\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}}(P(x)),\qquad x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2},

where
r^{\prime}:=\operatorname{rank}\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
,
n^{\prime}:=\dim\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}=r^{\prime}+\frac{d}{2}r^{\prime}(r^{\prime}-1)
.
The following subset
\Omega\subset\mathfrak{n}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle=(\text{connected component of }\{x\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}\mid P(x)\text{ is positive definite}\}\text{ which contains }e)

\displaystyle=(\text{connected component of }\{x\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}\mid\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)>0\}\text{ which contains }e)

is called the
symmetric cone
, and
(x|y)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}>0
holds for any
x,y\in\Omega
.

We say
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
is
invertible
if
P(x)
is invertible, and then the
inverse

x^{-\mathit{1}}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
is given by

x^{-\mathit{1}}:=P(x)^{-1}x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}.

Then
P(x)^{-1}=P(x^{-\mathit{1}})
holds. Also, for
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}(e)_{2}
, if
Q(x)|_{\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}(e)_{2}}
is invertible, then we set

{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx^{-\mathit{1}}:=\big(Q(x)|_{\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}(e)_{2}}\big)^{-1}x\in\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}(e)_{2}.

If
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is of tube type and if
e
is a maximal tripotent so that
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}=\mathfrak{n}^{+\mathbb{C}}
holds, then

{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx^{-\mathit{1}}\in\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}
does not depend on the choice of
e
,
and
{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx^{-\mathit{1}}=Q(e)x^{-\mathit{1}}
,
Q(x)^{-1}=Q\big({}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx^{-\mathit{1}}\big)
hold for
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
Also, for
x,y\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\mathfrak{n}^{+\mathbb{C}}
, if
y
is invertible then we have

\displaystyle P(x+y)=B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big({-}x,y^{-\mathit{1}}\big)P(y)=B\big({-}x,{}^{t}\hskip-1.0pty^{-\mathit{1}}\big)P(y),

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x+y)=h_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big({-}x,y^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(y)=h\big({-}x,{}^{t}\hskip-1.0pty^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(y).

(2.4)

Next we assume
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is of tube type, take two tripotents
e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
such that
D(e^{\prime},\overline{e^{\prime\prime}})=0
and
e=e^{\prime}+e^{\prime\prime}
is maximal,
and regard
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
as a Jordan algebra by using
e
. Let

\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}:=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{2}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime\prime})_{0},\qquad\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{12}:=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime\prime})_{1},\qquad\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}:=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{0}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime\prime})_{2},

so that
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
are Jordan subalgebras. We use the same notation
x^{-\mathit{1}}
to express the inverses in
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{jj}
.
Then for
x_{ij},y_{ij}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{ij}
we have

\displaystyle P(x_{11}+x_{22})y_{11}=P(x_{11})y_{11}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11},\qquad

\displaystyle P(x_{12})y_{11}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22},

\displaystyle P(x_{11}+x_{22})y_{12}=P(x_{11},x_{22})y_{12}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{12},\qquad

\displaystyle P(x_{12})y_{12}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{12},

\displaystyle P(x_{11}+x_{22})y_{22}=P(x_{22})y_{22}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22},\qquad

\displaystyle P(x_{12})y_{22}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}.

Moreover we have the following.

Lemma 2.1
.

For
x_{jj},y_{jj}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{jj}
,
z_{12}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{12}
, we have

1.

P(x_{11}+x_{22})z_{12}=P(x_{11},x_{22})z_{12}=4L(x_{11})L(x_{22})z_{12}=4L(x_{22})L(x_{11})z_{12}
.

2.

D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{11},y_{11})z_{12}=4L(x_{11})L(y_{11})z_{12}
,
D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{22},y_{22})z_{12}=4L(x_{22})L(y_{22})z_{12}
.

3.

4L(x_{11})L\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)z_{12}=z_{12}
,
4L(x_{22})L\big(x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)z_{12}=z_{12}
.

Proof.

(1) By the definition of
P
,
L
and
[
9
, Part V, Proposition I.2.1(J1.2)]
we have

\displaystyle P(x_{11},x_{22})z_{12}=Q(x_{11},x_{22})Q(\overline{e})z_{12}\!=Q(x_{11},x_{22})Q(\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{e^{\prime\prime}})z_{12}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=-D(\{x_{11},\overline{e^{\prime}},x_{22}\},\overline{e^{\prime\prime}})z_{12}+D(x_{11},\overline{e^{\prime}})D(x_{22},\overline{e^{\prime\prime}})z_{12}+D(x_{22},\overline{e^{\prime}})D(x_{11},\overline{e^{\prime\prime}})z_{12}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=D(x_{11},\overline{e^{\prime}})D(x_{22},\overline{e^{\prime\prime}})z_{12}=D(x_{11},\overline{e})D(x_{22},\overline{e})z_{12}=4L(x_{11})L(x_{22})z_{12}.

The last equality is also proved similarly.

(2) Similarly, by
[
9
, Part V, Proposition I.2.1(J2.1
′
)]
we have

\displaystyle D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{11},y_{11})z_{12}

\displaystyle=D(x_{11},Q(\overline{e})y_{11})z_{12}=D(x_{11},Q(\overline{e^{\prime}})y_{11})z_{12}

\displaystyle=D(x_{11},\overline{e^{\prime}})D(y_{11},\overline{e^{\prime}})z_{12}-Q(x_{11},y_{11})Q(\overline{e^{\prime}})z_{12}

\displaystyle=D(x_{11},\overline{e})D(y_{11},\overline{e})z_{12}=4L(x_{11})L(y_{11})z_{12}.

The 2nd formula is also proved similarly.

(3) By (1) we have

\displaystyle 4L(x_{11})L\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)z_{12}

\displaystyle=16L(x_{11})L(e^{\prime\prime})L\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)L(e^{\prime\prime})z_{12}=P(x_{11}+e^{\prime\prime})P\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}+e^{\prime\prime}\big)z_{12}

\displaystyle=P(x_{11}+e^{\prime\prime})P\big((x_{11}+e^{\prime\prime})^{-\mathit{1}}\big)z_{12}=z_{12}.

The 2nd formula is also proved similarly.
∎

2.3
Symmetric subalgebras of Jordan algebras

In this section we consider a
\mathbb{C}
-linear involution
\sigma
on a simple Hermitian positive Jordan triple system
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
, i.e., a Jordan triple system automorphism
\sigma\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\to\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
of order 2
which commutes with the
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear map
\bar{\cdot}\colon\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\to\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}
. We write

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big)^{\sigma}=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\mid\sigma(x)=x\big\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big)^{-\sigma}=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\mid\sigma(x)=-x\big\}.

First we prove the following.

Proposition 2.2
.

Assume
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is simple, and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
are of tube type.

1.

A maximal tripotent
e
of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
is also a maximal tripotent of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.

2.

If
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
is not simple, then there exists a tripotent
e^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
such that
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{0}
,

\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1}
hold.

Proof.

(1) Let
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{-\sigma}
be a maximal tripotent so that
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})^{-\sigma}
holds,
and take a tripotent
e^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0})^{\sigma}
such that
\tilde{e}=e+e^{\prime}
is a maximal tripotent of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
We regard
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
as a Jordan algebra by
\tilde{e}
. Then for
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{1}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{1})^{\sigma}
we have

x^{\mathit{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\{x,\overline{e}+\overline{e^{\prime}},x\}\in(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0})^{-\sigma}\oplus(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})^{\sigma}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})^{\sigma}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}.

On the other hand, let
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r^{\prime}}\}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
,
\{e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,e_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{\prime}\}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0}
be Jordan frames.
Then we have

\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{1}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r^{\prime}\\
1\leq j\leq r^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e_{i})_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e_{j}^{\prime})_{1},

and for
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e_{i})_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e_{j}^{\prime})_{1}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{1}
, by
[
11
, Proposition IV.1.4]
we have

x^{\mathit{2}}=\frac{1}{2}(x|x)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(e_{i}+e_{j}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0}.

Therefore we must have
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{1}=\{0\}
, so that
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0}
holds.
Hence
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is an ideal, and since
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is assumed to be simple, we have
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{0}=\{0\}
,
and therefore
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is a maximal tripotent.

(2) Let
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{-\sigma}
be non-trivial ideals such that
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
holds,
and let
e^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
,
e^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
be maximal tripotents of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
respectively.
Then
e=e^{\prime}+e^{\prime\prime}
is a maximal tripotent of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
. We regard
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
as a Jordan algebra by
e
.
Since
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
are also of tube type, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{2})^{-\sigma}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime\prime})_{0})^{-\sigma},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{0})^{-\sigma}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime\prime})_{2})^{-\sigma},

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1})^{-\sigma}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime\prime})_{1})^{-\sigma}=\{0\}.

Let
x_{2}\in(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{2})^{\sigma}
. Then for
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1})^{\sigma}
we have

L(x_{2})y_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\{x_{2},\overline{e},y_{1}\}\in(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1})^{-\sigma}=\{0\}.

Especially, for any
y_{1},w_{1}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1}
,
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{0}
, by Lemma
2.1
(1) we have

(P(y_{1},w_{1})z_{0}|x_{2})_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}=(P(z_{0},x_{2})y_{1}|w_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}=4(L(z_{0})L(x_{2})y_{1}|w_{1})_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}=0,

and since
P(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1},\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1})\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{0}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{2}
holds, we get
x_{2}=0
, and
(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{2})^{\sigma}=\{0\}
.
Similarly we have
(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{0})^{\sigma}=\{0\}
. Therefore we get
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{2}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{0}
and

\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{\sigma}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 2.3
.

For a simple Hermitian positive Jordan triple system
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
and an involution
\sigma
on
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
,

\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{-\sigma}
is a direct sum of at most two simple Jordan triple subsystems.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
are of tube type, then since
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{2}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{\prime})_{0}
are always simple for any tripotent
e^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
this follows from the previous proposition. If
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
are general,
let
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{k}\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2(j)}
be the simple decomposition, and take maximal tripotents
e_{j}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2(j)}
.
Then
e=\sum_{j=1}^{k}e_{j}
is a maximal tripotent of
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
, and we have

(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})^{-\sigma}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}(e)_{2}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{k}\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2(j)}(e)_{2}
. Therefore by the result for tube type case, we have
k\leq 2
.
∎

In the rest of this section, we assume
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
are of tube type, fix a maximal tripotent
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
and regard
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
as a Jordan algebra by
e
. If
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
is not simple, we also write
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
,

\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{12}
, and take maximal tripotents
e^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
,
e^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
such that
e=e^{\prime}+e^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)
,
\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{jj}}(x_{jj})
be the determinant polynomials on the Jordan algebras
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{jj}
,
and express the inverse on these by the same symbol
x^{-\mathit{1}}
,
x_{jj}^{-\mathit{1}}
.

Proposition 2.4
.

1.

For
x=x_{1}+x_{2}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
, assume
x_{2}
is invertible.
Then
x
is invertible if and only if
x_{2}-P(x_{1})x_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}
is invertible, and we have

\displaystyle x^{-\mathit{1}}=-P\big(x_{2}-P(x_{1})x_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-1}(x_{1}-P(x_{1})P(x_{2})^{-1}x_{1})+\big(x_{2}-P(x_{1})x_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)^{2}=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{2}-P(x_{1})x_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{2})=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(Q(x_{1}){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^+}(x)^2}{}=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(x_{1},Q(x_{2})^{-1}x_{1}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{2})^{2}.

2.

For
x=x_{11}+x_{12}+x_{22}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{12}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
, assume
x_{11}
and
x_{22}
are invertible
in
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
respectively. Then
x
is invertible if and only if

x_{11}-P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}
and
x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}
are invertible in
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
respectively, and we have

\displaystyle x^{-\mathit{1}}\!=\!\big(x_{11}\!-\!P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}\!\!-\!P\big(\big(x_{11}\!-\!P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}\!\!,x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}\!+\!\big(x_{22}\!-\!P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{x^{-\mathit{1}}}{}\!=\!\big(x_{11}\!-\!P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}\!\!-\!P\big(\big(x_{22}\!-\!P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}\!\!,x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}\!+\!\big(x_{22}\!-\!P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}\!,

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}\big(x_{11}-P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{22}}(x_{22})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^+}(x)}{}=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}}\big(Q(x_{12}){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{22}^{-\mathit{1}},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{22}}(x_{22})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^+}(x)}{}=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{22}}\big(x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^+}(x)
}=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}}\big(Q(x_{12}){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{22}}(x_{22}).

Proof.

(1) For the 1st formula, first we assume
x_{2}=e
. Then since
\mathbb{C}[x_{1}]\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is commutative and associative,

e-P(x_{1})e^{-\mathit{1}}=e-x_{1}^{\mathit{2}}=(e-x_{1})\cdot(e+x_{1})=-(x_{1}+e)\cdot\sigma(x_{1}+e)
holds,
and this is invertible if and only if
x_{1}+e
is invertible. Easily we have

\displaystyle(\text{RHS})

\displaystyle=-P\big(e-P(x_{1})e^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-1}(x_{1}-P(x_{1})x_{1})+\big(e-P(x_{1})e^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}

\displaystyle=-\big(e-x_{1}^{\mathit{2}}\big)^{\mathit{-2}}\cdot\big(x_{1}-x_{1}^{\mathit{3}}\big)+\big(e-x_{1}^{\mathit{2}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}=(x_{1}+e)^{-\mathit{1}}=(\text{LHS}).

Next we consider general invertible
x_{2}
. Then there exists
\sqrt{x_{2}}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
satisfying
(\sqrt{x_{2}})^{\mathit{2}}=x_{2}
(not unique).
Let
z_{1}:=P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}x_{1}
. Then we have

\displaystyle x_{2}-P(x_{1})x_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}

\displaystyle=P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)e-P\big(P(\sqrt{x_{2}})z_{1}\big)P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}e=P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)e-P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)P(z_{1})e

\displaystyle=P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)\big(e-z_{1}^{\mathit{2}}\big)=P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)\big((e-z_{1})\cdot(e+z_{1})\big),

and this is invertible if and only if
z_{1}+e=P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}(x_{1}+x_{2})
is invertible, or equivalently
x_{1}+x_{2}=x
is invertible. Moreover we have

\displaystyle(\text{RHS})={}

\displaystyle-P\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)e-P\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)z_{1}\big)P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}e\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\times\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)z_{1}-P\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)z_{1}\big)P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}z_{1}\big)

\displaystyle+\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)e-P\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)z_{1}\big)P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}e\big)^{-\mathit{1}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-P\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)e-P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)P(z_{1})e\big)^{-1}\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)z_{1}-P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)P(z_{1})z_{1}\big)

\displaystyle+\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)e-P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)P(z_{1})e\big)^{-\mathit{1}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}P(e-P(z_{1})e)^{-1}(z_{1}-P(z_{1})z_{1})+P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}(e-P(z_{1})e)^{-\mathit{1}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)^{-1}(z_{1}+e)^{-\mathit{1}}=\big(P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)z_{1}+P\big(\sqrt{x_{2}}\big)e\big)^{-\mathit{1}}=(x_{1}+x_{2})^{-\mathit{1}}=(\text{LHS}).

This proves the 1st formula.
Next we consider the 2nd formula. Since
-\sigma
fixes
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{2}
,
-\sigma
is an automorphism of the Jordan algebra,
and therefore preserves the determinant, that is,
\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{1}+x_{2})=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(-\sigma(x_{1}+x_{2}))=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(-x_{1}+x_{2})
holds.
Then by (
2.4
) and
[
9
, Part V, Proposition I.5.1(J4.2)]
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{1}+x_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{1}+x_{2})\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(-x_{1}+x_{2})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big({-}x_{1},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(x_{1},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(Q(x_{1}){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{2}-Q(x_{1}){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{2}-P(x_{1})x_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{2}),

and by
[
9
, Part V, Proposition I.5.1(J4.2), (J4.2
′
)]
, we have

\displaystyle h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(Q(x_{1}){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(x_{1},Q\big({}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{2}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{1}\big)=h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(x_{1},Q(x_{2})^{-1}x_{1}\big).

(2) For the 1st formulas, since

x_{11}-P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11},\qquad x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22},

by (1) with
x_{12}=x_{1}
,
x_{11}+x_{22}=x_{2}
and by Lemma
2.1
(1) we have

\displaystyle x^{-\mathit{1}}={}

\displaystyle-P\big(x_{11}+x_{22}-P(x_{12})\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}+x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\big)^{-1}\big(x_{12}-P(x_{12})P(x_{11}+x_{22})^{-1}x_{12}\big)

\displaystyle+\big(x_{11}+x_{22}-P(x_{12})\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}+x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\big)^{-\mathit{1}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-4L\big((x_{11}\!-P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}})^{-\mathit{1}}\big)L\big((x_{22}\!-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}})^{-\mathit{1}}\big)

\displaystyle{}\times\big(x_{12}\!-P(x_{12})P\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}\big)+\big(x_{11}-P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}+\big(x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}.

Then by
[
9
, Part V, (J1*) after Remark I.2.1]
and Lemma
2.1
(2), (3) we have

\displaystyle x_{12}-P(x_{12})P\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}

\displaystyle=x_{12}-D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}

\displaystyle=4L(x_{22})L\big(x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}-4L\big(P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)L\big(x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}

\displaystyle=4L(x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}})L\big(x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12},

and hence

\displaystyle 4L\big(\big(x_{11}-P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}\big)L\big(\big(x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\big(x_{12}-P(x_{12})P\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=4L\big(\big(x_{11}-P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}}\big)L\big(x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}=P\big(\big(x_{11}-P(x_{12})x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-\mathit{1}},x_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}.

This proves the 1st equality. The 2nd equality is also proved similarly.
Next we prove the 2nd formulas. To do this, we consider
B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
. Then this satisfies

\displaystyle\big(B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}})-I\big)y_{11}=-D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)y_{11}+P(x_{12})P\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)y_{11}\!\in\!\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{12}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22},\quad

\displaystyle y_{11}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11},

\displaystyle\big(B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)-I\big)y_{12}=-D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)y_{12}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22},\qquad

\displaystyle y_{12}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{12},

\displaystyle\big(B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)-I\big)y_{22}=0,\qquad

\displaystyle y_{22}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}.

Thus
B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)
is strictly lower-triangular if we choose a basis suitably,
and hence
\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\allowbreak\big(B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\big)=1
holds. Similarly,
B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},x_{12})
is strictly upper-triangular,
and hence
\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},x_{12}\big)\big)=1
holds.
Now since we have

\displaystyle B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)(x_{11}+x_{12}+x_{22})

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\big(I-D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)+P(x_{12})P\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\big)(x_{11}+x_{12}+x_{22})

\displaystyle\qquad{}=x_{11}+x_{12}+x_{22}-D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{11}-D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}+P(x_{12})P\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{11}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=x_{11}+x_{12}+x_{22}-D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{11},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)x_{12}-2P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}+P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=x_{11}+x_{12}+x_{22}-x_{12}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}=x_{11}+x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},

we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)^{\frac{2n}{r}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(P(x))=\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(P\big(B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-1}\big(x_{11}+x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{12},x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{-1}P\big(x_{11}+x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},x_{12}\big)^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Det}_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(P\big(x_{11}+x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{11}+x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)^{\frac{2n}{r}},

and since
x_{11}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
,
x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
hold, by (
2.4
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x_{11}+x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{22}}\big(x_{22}-P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{22}}(x_{22})h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}}\big(P(x_{12})x_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{11}}(x_{11})\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{22}}(x_{22})h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}}\big(Q(x_{12}){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{11}^{-\mathit{1}},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx_{22}^{-\mathit{1}}\big)

holds. The other equalities are proved similarly by interchanging
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{11}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{22}
.
∎

2.4
Structure groups and the Kantor–Koecher–Tits construction

Let
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
be a Hermitian positive Jordan triple system. For
l\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
,
let
\overline{l},{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptl\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})
,
l^{*}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
be the elements
satisfying
\overline{l}\overline{x}=\overline{lx}
,

(lx|\overline{y})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}=(x|{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptl\overline{y})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}=(x|\overline{l^{*}y})_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}

for any
x,y\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
Then the
structure group

\operatorname{Str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
is defined as

\operatorname{Str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}):=\big\{l\in GL_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})\mid\big\{lx,{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptl^{-1}y,lz\big\}=l\{x,y,z\},\ x,z\in\mathfrak{p}^{+},\,y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}\big\}.

Then for
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}
,
l\in\operatorname{Str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
we have

\displaystyle D(lx,y)=lD\big(x,{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptly\big)l^{-1},\qquad Q(lx)=lQ(x){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptl,

\displaystyle B(lx,y)=lB\big(x,{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptly\big)l^{-1},\qquad h(lx,y)=h\big(x,{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptly\big).

Also, for any
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}
,
B(x,y)\in\operatorname{Str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
holds if
B(x,y)
is invertible.
Let
\mathfrak{str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})=\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}
denote the Lie algebra of
\operatorname{Str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
.
Then
D(\mathfrak{p}^{+},\mathfrak{p}^{-})=\mathfrak{str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
and
I_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\in\mathfrak{str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
hold.

Next we construct a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
from
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
via the
Kantor–Koecher–Tits construction
. As a vector space let

\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathfrak{p}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{-},

and give the Lie algebra structure by

[(x,k,y),(z,l,w)]:=\big(kz-lx,[k,l]+D(x,w)-D(z,y),-{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptkw+{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptly\big).

Then
I_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\in\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
acts by
+1
,
0
and
-1
on each summand respectively.
Let
(\cdot|\cdot)_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}}\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C}
be the
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}
-invariant bilinear form normalized such that

(x|y)_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}}=(x|y)_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}
holds for any
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}
.
Next let
\hat{\vartheta}
be the involution on
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}
given by

\hat{\vartheta}(x,k,y):=(\overline{y},-k^{*},\overline{x}),

and let
\mathfrak{g}:=\big(\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)^{\hat{\vartheta}}
,
\mathfrak{k}:=\big(\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)^{\hat{\vartheta}}
.
We fix a connected complex Lie group
G^{\mathbb{C}}
with the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}
,
and let
G,K^{\mathbb{C}},K,P^{+},P^{-}\subset G^{\mathbb{C}}
be the connected closed subgroups corresponding to the Lie subalgebras

\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}^{+},\mathfrak{p}^{-}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}
respectively.
Then we have a covering map
{\rm Ad}|_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\colon K^{\mathbb{C}}\to\operatorname{Str}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})_{0}\subset\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
.
For
l\in K^{\mathbb{C}}
,
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
, we abbreviate
{\rm Ad}(l)x
as
lx
.

Next we fix a maximal tripotent
e\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
, let
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}(e)_{2}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
be as in (
2.2
),
take the corresponding Euclidean real form
\mathfrak{n}^{+}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
, and let
\mathfrak{n}^{-}:=\overline{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}=Q(\overline{e})\mathfrak{n}^{+}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{2}
.
Also, let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(e)_{2}:=D(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2},\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{2})=[\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2},\mathfrak{p}^{-}(e)_{2}]\subset\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}:=D(\mathfrak{n}^{+},\mathfrak{n}^{-})=[\mathfrak{n}^{+},\mathfrak{n}^{-}]=\big\{l\in\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(e)_{2}\mid l=Q(e)\overline{l}Q(\overline{e})\big\}\subset\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}.

Then these become subalgebras, and

{}^{c}\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{n}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{l}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{-}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}

also becomes a subalgebra. Let
K^{\mathbb{C}}(e)_{2}
,
{}^{c}\hskip-1.0ptG\subset G^{\mathbb{C}}
be the connected closed subgroups corresponding to

\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(e)_{2}
,
{}^{c}\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{g}
respectively,
and let
L:=K^{\mathbb{C}}\cap{}^{c}\hskip-1.0ptG
,
K_{L}:=L\cap K
,
\mathfrak{k}_{\mathfrak{l}}:=\mathfrak{l}\cap\mathfrak{k}
.
Then
L
acts transitively on the symmetric cone
\Omega\subset\mathfrak{n}^{+}
, and
K_{L}
acts on
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
as Jordan algebra automorphisms.
For
l\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})
, let
l^{\top}:=Q(e){}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptlQ(\overline{e})\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2})

so that
(lx|y)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}=(x|l^{\top}y)_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}
holds for
x,y\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
.
Then for
x,y\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e)_{2}
,
l\in K^{\mathbb{C}}(e)_{2}
we have

\displaystyle D_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(lx,y)=lD_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x,l^{\top}y\big)l^{-1},\qquad

\displaystyle P(lx)=lP(x)l^{\top},

\displaystyle B_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(lx,y)=lB_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\big(x,l^{\top}y\big)l^{-1},\qquad

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(lx)=\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(le)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x).

2.5
Root space decomposition

In this section we assume
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
is simple, or equivalently,
the corresponding Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
via the Kantor–Kocher–Tits construction is simple.
Let
r:=\operatorname{rank}\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{g}
, and fix a frame
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
Then
e:=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{r}
is a maximal tripotent, and we consider the corresponding subalgebras
\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}(e)_{2}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
,

\mathfrak{l}\subset\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(e)_{2}\subset\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}
as in the previous section.
Next let
h_{j}:=D(e_{j},\overline{e_{j}})=[e_{j},\overline{e_{j}}]\in\mathfrak{l}\subset\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Then

\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}:=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}\mathbb{R}h_{j}\subset\mathfrak{l}\subset\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}

is a maximal split abelian subalgebra of
\mathfrak{l}
. We take a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{k}
containing
\sqrt{-1}\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}
.
Then simultaneously
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbb{C}}
becomes a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}
.
We define the linear forms
\gamma_{i}\in\big(\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)^{\vee}
by
\gamma_{i}(h_{j})=2\delta_{ij}

(i=1,\dots,r)
and
\gamma_{i}|_{(\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\mathbb{C}})^{\bot}}=0
,
and we take a positive root system
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{+}=\Delta^{+}\big(\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)\subset\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}}=\Delta\big(\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)
such that
the restriction of
\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{+}
to
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\mathbb{C}}
sits in

\displaystyle\big\{\gamma_{i}|_{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\mathbb{C}}}\mid 1\leq i\leq r\big\}\cup\bigg\{\frac{\gamma_{i}\pm\gamma_{j}}{2}\bigg|_{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\mathbb{C}}}\,\bigg|\,1\leq i<j\leq r\bigg\}\cup\bigg\{\frac{\gamma_{i}}{2}\bigg|_{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\mathbb{C}}}\,\bigg|\,1\leq i\leq r\bigg\}\cup\{0\},

and set
\Delta_{\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}=\Delta\big(\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)
,

\Delta_{\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{+}=\Delta^{+}\big(\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{h}^{\mathbb{C}}\big)=\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{+}\cap\Delta_{\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}
.
Next, let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}_{ij}:=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\mid[h_{l},x]=\pm(\delta_{il}+\delta_{jl})x,\,l=1,\dots,r\big\},\qquad

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq j\leq r,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}_{0j}:=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\mid[h_{l},x]=\pm\delta_{jl}x,\,l=1,\dots,r\big\},\qquad

\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq r,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}_{ij}:=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\mid[h_{l},x]=(\delta_{il}-\delta_{jl})x,\,l=1,\dots,r\big\},\qquad

\displaystyle 1\leq i,j\leq r,\;i\neq j,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}_{i0}:=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\mid[h_{l},x]=\delta_{il}x,\,l=1,\dots,r\big\},\qquad

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq r,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}_{0j}:=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\mid[h_{l},x]=-\delta_{jl}x,\,l=1,\dots,r\big\},\qquad

\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq r,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\mid[h_{l},x]=0,\;(h_{l}|x)_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}}=0,\,l=1,\dots,r\big\},\qquad

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}_{ij}:=\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}_{ij}\cap\mathfrak{l},\qquad

\displaystyle 1\leq i,j\leq r,\quad i\neq j,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{l}}:=\mathfrak{m}^{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathfrak{l},

so that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq j\leq r\\
(i,j)\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}_{ij},\qquad\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{m}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i,j\leq r\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}_{ij},\qquad\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}\oplus\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{l}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq r\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{l}_{ij}

hold. We set

\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{l}}:=\bigoplus_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\mathfrak{l}_{ij},\qquad\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\top}:=\bigoplus_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\mathfrak{l}_{ji},

let
A_{L},N_{L},N_{L}^{\top}\subset L
be the connected closed subgroups corresponding to the Lie subalgebras
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{l}}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{l}}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\top}

respectively, and let

M_{L}:=\{k\in K_{L}\mid{\rm Ad}(k)h_{l}=0,\,l=1,\dots,r\},

so that
L=K_{L}A_{L}N_{L}=K_{L}A_{L}N_{L}^{\top}
holds and
M_{L}A_{L}N_{L},M_{L}A_{L}N_{L}^{\top}\subset L
are minimal parabolic subgroups.
Then
A_{L}N_{L},A_{L}N_{L}^{\top}\subset L
acts on
\Omega
simply transitively.

2.6
Polynomials and hypergeometric series on Jordan triple systems

For a Hermitian positive Jordan triple system
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
, let
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
be the space of holomorphic polynomials on
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
.
Then
K^{\mathbb{C}}
acts on
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
by

\displaystyle({\rm Ad}|_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(l))^{\vee}f(x)=f\big(l^{-1}x\big),\qquad

\displaystyle l\in K^{\mathbb{C}},\qquad

\displaystyle f\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+},

\displaystyle({\rm Ad}|_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}(l))^{\vee}f(y)=f\big({}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptly\big),\qquad

\displaystyle l\in K^{\mathbb{C}},\qquad

\displaystyle f\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{-}),\qquad

\displaystyle y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}.

We assume
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is simple, fix a frame
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
and consider tripotents
e^{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}e_{i}
for
k=1,2,\dots,r
. Then the subalgebra

\mathfrak{p}^{+}\big(e^{k}\big)_{2}=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq j\leq k}\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{ij}

is of tube type and has a Jordan algebra structure of rank
k
.
Let
\Delta_{k}(x)
be the determinant polynomial on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}(e^{k})_{2}
, and extend to a polynomial on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
Using this, for
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
we define the function
\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}(x)
on the symmetric cone
\Omega\subset\mathfrak{n}^{+}
by

\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}(x):=\Delta_{1}(x)^{m_{1}-m_{2}}\Delta_{2}(x)^{m_{2}-m_{3}}\cdots\Delta_{r-1}(x)^{m_{r-1}-m_{r}}\Delta_{r}(x)^{m_{r}},\qquad x\in\Omega.

Then for
m\in M_{L}
,
a={\rm e}^{t_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+t_{r}h_{r}}\in A_{L}
,
n\in N_{L}
and for
x\in\Omega
, we have

\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}\big((man)^{\top}x\big)=\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}\big((man)^{\top}e\big)\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}(x)={\rm e}^{2t_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+2t_{r}m_{r}}\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}(x).

Especially, if

\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}:=\big\{\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}\mid m_{1}\geq\cdots\geq m_{r}\geq 0\big\},

then
\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}(x)
is extended to a polynomial on
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}):=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\big\{\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}\big(l^{-1}x\big)\mid l\in K^{\mathbb{C}}\big\}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{+}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}(\mathfrak{p}^{-}):=\big\{\overline{f(\overline{y})}\mid f(x)\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})\big\}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{-}).

Then we have the following.

Theorem 2.5

(Hua–Kostant–Schmid
[
9
, Part III, Theorem V.2.1]
)
.

Under the
K^{\mathbb{C}}
-action,
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
is decomposed as

\mathcal{P}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big)=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}_{++}}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big).

Each
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
is irreducible,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
has the lowest weight
-(m_{1}\gamma_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}\gamma_{r})
,
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})
has the highest weight
m_{1}\gamma_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}\gamma_{r}
.

In addition, if
\mathfrak{p}^{+}
is of tube type, then for

\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{r}:=\big\{\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}\mid m_{1}\geq\cdots\geq m_{r}\big\},

let

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big):=\mathcal{P}_{(m_{1}-m_{r},m_{2}-m_{r},\dots,m_{r-1}-m_{r},0)}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big)\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}}(x)^{m_{r}}\subset\mathcal{P}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big)\big[\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}}(x)^{-1}\big].

Then for
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{r}
we have

\big\{f\big({}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptx^{-\mathit{1}}\big)\mid f\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big)\big\}=\mathcal{P}_{-\mathbf{m}^{\vee}}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}\big),

where
\mathbf{m}^{\vee}:=(m_{r},\dots,m_{1})
.

Next, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
,
\mathbf{m}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{r}
,
d\in\mathbb{C}
, let

\displaystyle(\lambda+\mathbf{s})_{\mathbf{m},d}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\bigg(\lambda+s_{j}-\frac{d}{2}(j-1)\bigg)_{m_{j}},\qquad(\lambda)_{\mathbf{m},d}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\bigg(\lambda-\frac{d}{2}(j-1)\bigg)_{m_{j}},

(2.5)

where
(\lambda)_{m}=\lambda(\lambda+1)\cdots(\lambda+m-1)
, and let
\dim\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})=:d_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}
.
Using these, for
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}
, let
\Phi_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x),\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{K_{L}}
be the polynomials given by

\Phi_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x):=\int_{K_{L}}\Delta_{\mathbf{m}}(kx)\,{\rm d}k,\qquad\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x):=\frac{d_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}}{\left(\frac{n}{r}\right)_{\mathbf{m},d}}\Phi_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x),

(2.6)

and for
(t_{1},\dots,t_{r})\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
, define the symmetric polynomials
\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r})
by

\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r}):=\tilde{\Phi}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1}e_{1}+\cdots+t_{r}e_{r}).

(2.7)

This does not depend on the choice of Jordan frames
\{e_{j}\}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
.
Moreover, this does not depend on
r
, that is, if we construct
\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r})
,
\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r-1})

from two simple Jordan triple systems of rank
r
and
r-1
with common
d
, then we have

\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r-1},0)=\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r-1}).

Next we define the polynomials
\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}(x,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\big)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{m}}\big(\mathfrak{p}^{\mp}\big)
by

\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}(x,y):=\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r}),

(2.8)

by using the roots
t_{1},\dots,t_{r}
of
t^{r}h_{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}\big(t^{-1}x,y\big)\in\mathbb{C}[t]
. Then these satisfy

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(x,y)=\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}(y,x),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+},\qquad

\displaystyle y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-},

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(lx,y)=\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\big(x,{}^{t}\hskip-1.0ptly\big),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+},\quad

\displaystyle y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-},\quad l\in K^{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(x,\overline{e})=\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}.

For these polynomials we have

\displaystyle{\rm e}^{\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x)}=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+},

(2.9)

\displaystyle{\rm e}^{(x|y)_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}}=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(x,y),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+},\quad y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-},

\displaystyle{\rm e}^{t_{1}+\cdots+t_{r}}=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{d}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r}),\qquad

\displaystyle t_{j}\in\mathbb{C},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(e-x)^{-\lambda}=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}(\lambda)_{\mathbf{m},d}\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(x),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in D\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+},

\displaystyle h_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(x,y)^{-\lambda}=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}(\lambda)_{\mathbf{m},d}\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}(x,y),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in D\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+},\quad y\in\overline{D}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{-},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1-t_{j})^{-\lambda}=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}(\lambda)_{\mathbf{m},d}\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{m}}^{d}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r}),\qquad

\displaystyle t_{j}\in\mathbb{C},\quad|t_{j}|<1

(2.12)

(see
[
11
, Proposition XII.1.3]
,
[
9
, Part III, Theorem V.3.10]
). Using these, for
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{C}

with
\gamma\notin\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}\left(\frac{d}{2}(j-1)-\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right)
, let

\displaystyle{}_{0}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(\gamma;x):=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\frac{1}{(\gamma)_{\mathbf{m},d}}\tilde{\Phi}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}_{\mathbf{m}}(x),

\displaystyle{}_{0}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}(\gamma;x,y):=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\frac{1}{(\gamma)_{\mathbf{m},d}}\tilde{\Phi}^{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}_{\mathbf{m}}(x,y),

(2.13)

\displaystyle{}_{0}F_{1}^{d}(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{r}):=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\frac{1}{(\gamma)_{\mathbf{m},d}}\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r}),

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};x\right):=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\frac{(\alpha)_{\mathbf{m},d}(\beta)_{\mathbf{m},d}}{(\gamma)_{\mathbf{m},d}}\tilde{\Phi}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}_{\mathbf{m}}(x),

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};x,y\right):=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\frac{(\alpha)_{\mathbf{m},d}(\beta)_{\mathbf{m},d}}{(\gamma)_{\mathbf{m},d}}\tilde{\Phi}^{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}_{\mathbf{m}}(x,y),

(2.14)

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}^{d}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};t_{1},\dots,t_{r}\right):=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}}\frac{(\alpha)_{\mathbf{m},d}(\beta)_{\mathbf{m},d}}{(\gamma)_{\mathbf{m},d}}\tilde{\Phi}^{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{r}).

{}_{0}F_{1}
converges for all
x\in\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
y\in\mathfrak{p}^{-}
or
t_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
and
{}_{2}F_{1}
converges if
x\in D\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
y\in\overline{D}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{-}
or
|t_{j}|<1
.
Especially if
\alpha=-k\in-\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
then
{}_{2}F_{1}^{d}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-k,\beta\\
\gamma\end{smallmatrix};t_{1},\dots,t_{r}\right)

becomes a polynomial on
\mathbb{C}^{r}
of degree
kr
, and is well-defined for

\gamma\notin\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}\big(\frac{d}{2}(j-1)-\{0,1,\dots,k\}\big)
.
In this paper we use the same notation
{}_{2}F_{1}^{d}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-k,\beta\\
\gamma\end{smallmatrix};t_{1},\dots,t_{r}\right)

for this polynomial even if
\gamma
is a negative (half) integer except for the above set,
and similar for
{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}
,
{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}}
.
By
[
11
, Proposition XV.3.4]
, for
t_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
|t_{j}|<1
we have

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}^{d}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};t_{1},\dots,t_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1-t_{j})^{-\alpha}{}_{2}F_{1}^{d}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\gamma-\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};\frac{t_{1}}{t_{1}-1},\dots,\frac{t_{r}}{t_{r}-1}\right)

(2.15)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1-t_{j})^{\gamma-\alpha-\beta}{}_{2}F_{1}^{d}\left(\begin{matrix}\gamma-\alpha,\gamma-\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};t_{1},\dots,t_{r}\right)\!.

(2.16)

By this formula we can show that

{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};x,y\right)=h(x,y)^{\gamma-\alpha-\beta}{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\left(\begin{matrix}\gamma-\alpha,\gamma-\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};x,y\right)

holds for
x\in D\subset\mathfrak{p}^{+}
,
y\in\overline{D}\subset\mathfrak{p}^{-}
.
Next, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let

\underline{k}_{r}:=(\underbrace{k,\dots,k}_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}^{r}.

Then the following formulas hold.

Proposition 2.6
.

We assume
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\mathfrak{n}^{+\mathbb{C}}
is of tube type.

1.

For
\alpha=-k\in-\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\left(\begin{matrix}-k,\beta\\
\gamma\end{matrix};x\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\beta)_{\underline{k}_{r},d}}{(\gamma)_{\underline{k}_{r},d}}\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(-x)^{k}{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\left(\begin{matrix}-k,-k-\gamma+\frac{n}{r}\\
-k-\beta+\frac{n}{r}\end{matrix};x^{-\mathit{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\beta)_{\underline{k}_{r},d}}{(\gamma)_{\underline{k}_{r},d}}\operatorname{det}_{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}(e-x)^{k}{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\left(\begin{matrix}-k,\gamma-\beta\\
-k-\beta+\frac{n}{r}\end{matrix};(e-x)^{-\mathit{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\gamma-\beta)_{\underline{k}_{r},d}}{(\gamma)_{\underline{k}_{r},d}}{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\left(\begin{matrix}-k,\beta\\
-k+\beta-\gamma+\frac{n}{r}\end{matrix};e-x\right)\!.

2.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then we have

\lim_{\gamma\to\frac{n}{r}-k}(\gamma)_{\underline{k}_{r},d}\,{}_{2}F_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}^{+}}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\beta\\
